What brings you in today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 46 / no_occupation / some_college\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Weight_Appetite_Change\nmanifestation: \"What finally pushed me to come in is that my eating has been all over the place—some weeks I barely touch dinner, and other nights I stand in the kitchen eating crackers and sweets after everyone’s gone to bed.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I don’t know if that’s the sort of thing you mean, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nDoctor: When you eat in the kitchen late at night, does it feel like you can’t stop or control how much you’re eating?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say it’s completely out of control, but once I’m there eating crackers or sweets, I can kind of keep going longer than I meant to. It’s usually after everyone’s gone to bed, and then I feel a little embarrassed about it. I’m not sure if that counts as not being able to stop.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, have you usually been asleep first and then wake up to eat?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. It’s more like I’m already awake later than I should be, and then I end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and sometimes it turns into more than I meant. But I don’t think I’m waking up from sleep to do it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: After you eat more than you meant to, do you ever make yourself vomit?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up after. I might feel bad or kind of embarrassed that I ate more than I meant to, but I haven’t done that.\n\nDoctor: After you eat more than you meant to, do you ever try to make up for it by using laxatives?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I haven’t used laxatives to make up for eating. I mostly just feel guilty afterward and tell myself I’ll do better the next day.\n\nDoctor: How much does your body weight or shape affect how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t love it when my clothes feel tight, and I can get kind of down on myself about it. It’s more like I’m already awake later than I should be, and then I end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and sometimes it turns into more than I meant. But I don’t think I’m waking up from sleep to do it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: After you eat more than you meant to, do you ever make yourself vomit?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up after. I might feel bad or kind of embarrassed that I ate more than I meant to, but I haven’t done that.\n\nDoctor: After you eat more than you meant to, do you ever try to make up for it by using laxatives?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I haven’t used laxatives to make up for eating. I mostly just feel guilty afterward and tell myself I’ll do better the next day.\n\nDoctor: How much does your body weight or shape affect how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t love it when my clothes feel tight, and I can get kind of down on myself about it. But I don’t think it’s, like, the main thing that decides how I feel about myself. I try not to make too big a deal of it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you eat what most people would consider a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… I mean, I don’t know if it would look huge to someone else, but there are nights where after everyone’s gone to bed I’ll stand in the kitchen and keep eating crackers or sweets, more than I meant to. Other weeks I barely touch dinner, so it’s kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night episodes, do you ever eat an amount of food that most people would clearly consider unusually large?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes it might be more than normal, yeah. I don’t know if it would be “clearly unusually large,” exactly. It’s more like I’m standing there after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, and I keep going even though I meant to just have a little. Then other weeks I barely eat dinner, so it feels kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Are you deliberately trying to lose weight by eating very little?\n\nPatient: Not really deliberately, no. I mean… some weeks I barely touch dinner, but it’s not like I’m on a strict diet or trying hard to lose weight. It’s more that my eating has just been kind of all over the place. Then other nights I’m in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets, and I feel bad about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every single day, but I do get stretches where it feels that way. The house just feels kind of gray and heavy, and I’ll sit at the kitchen table feeling empty, even if nothing bad really happened that day. I don’t know if that counts as depressed most days, but it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a strong way. I mean, I don’t love the idea of my clothes feeling tighter, but I wouldn’t say I’m really afraid of gaining weight. It’s more just… I notice it and then feel a little bad, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s usually after everyone’s gone to bed, and then I feel a little embarrassed about it. I’m not sure if that counts as not being able to stop.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, have you usually been asleep first and then wake up to eat?\n\nPatient: Not usually, no. It’s more like I’m already awake later than I should be, and then I end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and sometimes it turns into more than I meant. But I don’t think I’m waking up from sleep to do it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: After you eat more than you meant to, do you ever make yourself vomit?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up after. I might feel bad or kind of embarrassed that I ate more than I meant to, but I haven’t done that.\n\nDoctor: After you eat more than you meant to, do you ever try to make up for it by using laxatives?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I haven’t used laxatives to make up for eating. I mostly just feel guilty afterward and tell myself I’ll do better the next day.\n\nDoctor: How much does your body weight or shape affect how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t love it when my clothes feel tight, and I can get kind of down on myself about it. But I don’t think it’s, like, the main thing that decides how I feel about myself. I try not to make too big a deal of it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you eat what most people would consider a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… I mean, I don’t know if it would look huge to someone else, but there are nights where after everyone’s gone to bed I’ll stand in the kitchen and keep eating crackers or sweets, more than I meant to. Other weeks I barely touch dinner, so it’s kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night episodes, do you ever eat an amount of food that most people would clearly consider unusually large?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes it might be more than normal, yeah. I don’t know if it would be “clearly unusually large,” exactly. It’s more like I’m standing there after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, and I keep going even though I meant to just have a little. Then other weeks I barely eat dinner, so it feels kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Are you deliberately trying to lose weight by eating very little?\n\nPatient: Not really deliberately, no. I mean… some weeks I barely touch dinner, but it’s not like I’m on a strict diet or trying hard to lose weight. It’s more that my eating has just been kind of all over the place. Then other nights I’m in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets, and I feel bad about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every single day, but I do get stretches where it feels that way. The house just feels kind of gray and heavy, and I’ll sit at the kitchen table feeling empty, even if nothing bad really happened that day. I don’t know if that counts as depressed most days, but it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a strong way. I mean, I don’t love the idea of my clothes feeling tighter, but I wouldn’t say I’m really afraid of gaining weight. It’s more just… I notice it and then feel a little bad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel fat even when other people tell you that you are thin or look normal?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I can be critical of myself in the mirror sometimes, like anyone, I guess. But if someone says I look normal or thin, I don’t usually think they’re lying or anything. I might still feel a little uncomfortable in my body, but I wouldn’t say I feel fat exactly. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been losing your appetite?\n\nPatient: Kind of… yes. It’s not, like, constant, but my eating has been really uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’ll end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or sweets. I know that probably doesn’t sound great… but that’s been part of what made me think I should come in.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I try not to make too big a deal of it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you eat what most people would consider a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… I mean, I don’t know if it would look huge to someone else, but there are nights where after everyone’s gone to bed I’ll stand in the kitchen and keep eating crackers or sweets, more than I meant to. Other weeks I barely touch dinner, so it’s kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night episodes, do you ever eat an amount of food that most people would clearly consider unusually large?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes it might be more than normal, yeah. I don’t know if it would be “clearly unusually large,” exactly. It’s more like I’m standing there after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, and I keep going even though I meant to just have a little. Then other weeks I barely eat dinner, so it feels kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Are you deliberately trying to lose weight by eating very little?\n\nPatient: Not really deliberately, no. I mean… some weeks I barely touch dinner, but it’s not like I’m on a strict diet or trying hard to lose weight. It’s more that my eating has just been kind of all over the place. Then other nights I’m in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets, and I feel bad about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every single day, but I do get stretches where it feels that way. The house just feels kind of gray and heavy, and I’ll sit at the kitchen table feeling empty, even if nothing bad really happened that day. I don’t know if that counts as depressed most days, but it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a strong way. I mean, I don’t love the idea of my clothes feeling tighter, but I wouldn’t say I’m really afraid of gaining weight. It’s more just… I notice it and then feel a little bad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel fat even when other people tell you that you are thin or look normal?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I can be critical of myself in the mirror sometimes, like anyone, I guess. But if someone says I look normal or thin, I don’t usually think they’re lying or anything. I might still feel a little uncomfortable in my body, but I wouldn’t say I feel fat exactly. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been losing your appetite?\n\nPatient: Kind of… yes. It’s not, like, constant, but my eating has been really uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’ll end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or sweets. I know that probably doesn’t sound great… but that’s been part of what made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but things I used to really look forward to—like book club, cooking, going to galleries, even taking care of the rose beds—sometimes just feel like chores now. I try not to make too big a deal of it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you eat what most people would consider a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… I mean, I don’t know if it would look huge to someone else, but there are nights where after everyone’s gone to bed I’ll stand in the kitchen and keep eating crackers or sweets, more than I meant to. Other weeks I barely touch dinner, so it’s kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night episodes, do you ever eat an amount of food that most people would clearly consider unusually large?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes it might be more than normal, yeah. I don’t know if it would be “clearly unusually large,” exactly. It’s more like I’m standing there after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, and I keep going even though I meant to just have a little. Then other weeks I barely eat dinner, so it feels kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Are you deliberately trying to lose weight by eating very little?\n\nPatient: Not really deliberately, no. I mean… some weeks I barely touch dinner, but it’s not like I’m on a strict diet or trying hard to lose weight. It’s more that my eating has just been kind of all over the place. Then other nights I’m in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets, and I feel bad about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every single day, but I do get stretches where it feels that way. The house just feels kind of gray and heavy, and I’ll sit at the kitchen table feeling empty, even if nothing bad really happened that day. I don’t know if that counts as depressed most days, but it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a strong way. I mean, I don’t love the idea of my clothes feeling tighter, but I wouldn’t say I’m really afraid of gaining weight. It’s more just… I notice it and then feel a little bad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel fat even when other people tell you that you are thin or look normal?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I can be critical of myself in the mirror sometimes, like anyone, I guess. But if someone says I look normal or thin, I don’t usually think they’re lying or anything. I might still feel a little uncomfortable in my body, but I wouldn’t say I feel fat exactly. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been losing your appetite?\n\nPatient: Kind of… yes. It’s not, like, constant, but my eating has been really uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’ll end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or sweets. I know that probably doesn’t sound great… but that’s been part of what made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but things I used to really look forward to—like book club, cooking, going to galleries, even taking care of the rose beds—sometimes just feel like chores now. I keep putting them off, even though I know I usually like them. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: During those late-night eating episodes, do you feel unable to stop eating once you’ve started?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say unable to stop, exactly. That sounds a little dramatic. It’s more like once I’m standing there after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, I keep picking at things longer than I meant to. And then I feel embarrassed about it. But it’s not every night—my eating has just been really all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights it’s that late-night kitchen thing. Does that answer it okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I might feel bad or kind of embarrassed that I ate more than I meant to, but I haven’t done that.\n\nDoctor: After you eat more than you meant to, do you ever try to make up for it by using laxatives?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I haven’t used laxatives to make up for eating. I mostly just feel guilty afterward and tell myself I’ll do better the next day.\n\nDoctor: How much does your body weight or shape affect how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t love it when my clothes feel tight, and I can get kind of down on myself about it. But I don’t think it’s, like, the main thing that decides how I feel about myself. I try not to make too big a deal of it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you eat what most people would consider a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… I mean, I don’t know if it would look huge to someone else, but there are nights where after everyone’s gone to bed I’ll stand in the kitchen and keep eating crackers or sweets, more than I meant to. Other weeks I barely touch dinner, so it’s kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night episodes, do you ever eat an amount of food that most people would clearly consider unusually large?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes it might be more than normal, yeah. I don’t know if it would be “clearly unusually large,” exactly. It’s more like I’m standing there after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, and I keep going even though I meant to just have a little. Then other weeks I barely eat dinner, so it feels kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Are you deliberately trying to lose weight by eating very little?\n\nPatient: Not really deliberately, no. I mean… some weeks I barely touch dinner, but it’s not like I’m on a strict diet or trying hard to lose weight. It’s more that my eating has just been kind of all over the place. Then other nights I’m in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets, and I feel bad about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every single day, but I do get stretches where it feels that way. The house just feels kind of gray and heavy, and I’ll sit at the kitchen table feeling empty, even if nothing bad really happened that day. I don’t know if that counts as depressed most days, but it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a strong way. I mean, I don’t love the idea of my clothes feeling tighter, but I wouldn’t say I’m really afraid of gaining weight. It’s more just… I notice it and then feel a little bad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel fat even when other people tell you that you are thin or look normal?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I can be critical of myself in the mirror sometimes, like anyone, I guess. But if someone says I look normal or thin, I don’t usually think they’re lying or anything. I might still feel a little uncomfortable in my body, but I wouldn’t say I feel fat exactly. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been losing your appetite?\n\nPatient: Kind of… yes. It’s not, like, constant, but my eating has been really uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’ll end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or sweets. I know that probably doesn’t sound great… but that’s been part of what made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but things I used to really look forward to—like book club, cooking, going to galleries, even taking care of the rose beds—sometimes just feel like chores now. I keep putting them off, even though I know I usually like them. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: During those late-night eating episodes, do you feel unable to stop eating once you’ve started?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say unable to stop, exactly. And then I feel embarrassed about it. But it’s not every night—my eating has just been really all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights it’s that late-night kitchen thing. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have those depressed stretches lasted at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I’ve tried not to count it too closely, because it makes me feel a little guilty, but those gray periods where I’m not enjoying things and I feel bad about myself… they’ve definitely lasted more than two weeks at a time. Altogether, it’s probably been around seven months, if I’m being honest. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s more like I’m already awake later than I should be, and then I end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed. I’ll start with crackers or something sweet, and sometimes it turns into more than I meant. But I don’t think I’m waking up from sleep to do it, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: After you eat more than you meant to, do you ever make yourself vomit?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t make myself throw up after. I might feel bad or kind of embarrassed that I ate more than I meant to, but I haven’t done that.\n\nDoctor: After you eat more than you meant to, do you ever try to make up for it by using laxatives?\n\nPatient: Um… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I know what you’re asking, but I haven’t used laxatives to make up for eating. I mostly just feel guilty afterward and tell myself I’ll do better the next day.\n\nDoctor: How much does your body weight or shape affect how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t love it when my clothes feel tight, and I can get kind of down on myself about it. But I don’t think it’s, like, the main thing that decides how I feel about myself. I try not to make too big a deal of it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you eat what most people would consider a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yeah… I mean, I don’t know if it would look huge to someone else, but there are nights where after everyone’s gone to bed I’ll stand in the kitchen and keep eating crackers or sweets, more than I meant to. Other weeks I barely touch dinner, so it’s kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: During those late-night episodes, do you ever eat an amount of food that most people would clearly consider unusually large?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes it might be more than normal, yeah. I don’t know if it would be “clearly unusually large,” exactly. It’s more like I’m standing there after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, and I keep going even though I meant to just have a little. Then other weeks I barely eat dinner, so it feels kind of all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Are you deliberately trying to lose weight by eating very little?\n\nPatient: Not really deliberately, no. I mean… some weeks I barely touch dinner, but it’s not like I’m on a strict diet or trying hard to lose weight. It’s more that my eating has just been kind of all over the place. Then other nights I’m in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets, and I feel bad about it. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling depressed most days?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, not every single day, but I do get stretches where it feels that way. The house just feels kind of gray and heavy, and I’ll sit at the kitchen table feeling empty, even if nothing bad really happened that day. I don’t know if that counts as depressed most days, but it’s been there.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a strong way. I mean, I don’t love the idea of my clothes feeling tighter, but I wouldn’t say I’m really afraid of gaining weight. It’s more just… I notice it and then feel a little bad, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel fat even when other people tell you that you are thin or look normal?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I can be critical of myself in the mirror sometimes, like anyone, I guess. But if someone says I look normal or thin, I don’t usually think they’re lying or anything. I might still feel a little uncomfortable in my body, but I wouldn’t say I feel fat exactly. Is that… is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been losing your appetite?\n\nPatient: Kind of… yes. It’s not, like, constant, but my eating has been really uneven. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights I’ll end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed eating crackers or sweets. I know that probably doesn’t sound great… but that’s been part of what made me think I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but things I used to really look forward to—like book club, cooking, going to galleries, even taking care of the rose beds—sometimes just feel like chores now. I keep putting them off, even though I know I usually like them. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: During those late-night eating episodes, do you feel unable to stop eating once you’ve started?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know if I’d say unable to stop, exactly. That sounds a little dramatic. It’s more like once I’m standing there after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets, I keep picking at things longer than I meant to. And then I feel embarrassed about it. But it’s not every night—my eating has just been really all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights it’s that late-night kitchen thing. Does that answer it okay?\n\nDoctor: Have those depressed stretches lasted at least two weeks at a time?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I’ve tried not to count it too closely, because it makes me feel a little guilty, but those gray periods where I’m not enjoying things and I feel bad about myself… they’ve definitely lasted more than two weeks at a time. Altogether, it’s probably been around seven months, if I’m being honest. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, have you been sleeping much more or much less than usual?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really tracked my sleep closely enough to say it’s clearly much more or much less. Some nights feel off, but I don’t know if it’s enough to count as a real change. Is that okay to say?\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, have you been feeling tired or low on energy nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say “nearly every day.” I mean, I do feel kind of worn down sometimes during those periods, but I don’t know if it’s more than what anyone would feel when they’re in a slump. I haven’t really paid attention to it in a specific way. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, have you been feeling worthless or very guilty?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I do get really stuck on things. Like if I buy an expensive tube of pigment, or if I snap at my spouse, I can replay it for hours and feel like I’ve somehow ruined the whole household over it. I know that probably sounds excessive… but in the moment it feels really true. Is that what you mean by guilty?\n\nDoctor: During those gray periods, have you had trouble concentrating, like finding it hard to read, follow conversations, or stay focused?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it real trouble concentrating. I mean, sometimes I’m a little distracted or I have to reread something, but I haven’t really noticed a clear pattern during the gray periods. Conversations are mostly okay, I think. I’m not sure if that answers it the way you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
